========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, so… the main thing that kind of pushed me to finally make an appointment is work. I’m a claims processing clerk, and I’m supposed to be at my workstation reviewing claims and policies and just getting through them steadily. But I keep getting up during the day, like leaving my desk when I know I really need to stay there and finish the claim reviews.

It’s not even always for some important reason. Sometimes I’ll just stand up or walk away, and then I’m like, “Why did I do that? I need to be at my station.” It’s been bothering me because I know what I’m supposed to do, and I’m trying to do it, but staying put long enough to get through the work has been harder than it should be. That’s probably the biggest reason I’m here.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more like… I just end up getting away from what I’m supposed to be doing. I wouldn’t describe it as, like, this strong physical urge where my body has to move or I’m crawling out of my skin. It’s more that I’m sitting there with the claims open, and I know I’m supposed to keep reviewing them, and then somehow I’m standing up or walking off from my workstation.

And that’s really the thing that pushed me to come in, because it keeps happening during the day even when I know I need to stay put and finish the claim reviews. I’ll be aware afterward, like, “Okay, I shouldn’t have left my desk, I need to get back to this,” but in the moment it’s like I drift away from the task and then I’m not where I’m supposed to be.

Yeah, looking back, I don’t think this is totally brand new. I mean, it’s more obvious now because work has such specific expectations, like I’m supposed to sit there and move through claims in a steady way, but when I think back to school, there were definitely signs.

In elementary school especially, I remember teachers writing things like, I was bright or capable, but I’d lose worksheets, or I’d miss instructions, or I’d need reminders to actually finish the classwork. Like I wasn’t trying to be difficult, and I don’t remember thinking I didn’t care. It was more that I’d somehow not have the paper I needed, or I’d realize everyone else had started something and I hadn’t fully caught what we were supposed to do, or the teacher would have to come by and nudge me to finish.

So… yeah, I guess it’s not like it appeared out of nowhere recently. It feels like the same kind of pattern, just now it’s causing bigger problems because I’m not a kid in a classroom anymore.

But I keep getting up during the day, like leaving my desk when I know I really need to stay there and finish the claim reviews.\n\nIt’s not even always for some important reason. Sometimes I’ll just stand up or walk away, and then I’m like, “Why did I do that? I need to be at my station.” It’s been bothering me because I know what I’m supposed to do, and I’m trying to do it, but staying put long enough to get through the work has been harder than it should be. That’s probably the biggest reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: When you get up from your desk, do you notice an inner feeling of restlessness or a physical urge to move that makes it hard to sit still, or does it feel more like you just lose track of what you're doing and end up wandering off?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like… I just end up getting away from what I’m supposed to be doing. I wouldn’t describe it as, like, this strong physical urge where my body has to move or I’m crawling out of my skin. It’s more that I’m sitting there with the claims open, and I know I’m supposed to keep reviewing them, and then somehow I’m standing up or walking off from my workstation.\n\nAnd that’s really the thing that pushed me to come in, because it keeps happening during the day even when I know I need to stay put and finish the claim reviews. I’ll be aware afterward, like, “Okay, I shouldn’t have left my desk, I need to get back to this,” but in the moment it’s like I drift away from the task and then I’m not where I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, did you notice similar problems with staying focused or sticking to tasks when you were a kid or teenager, or is this something that's really only become an issue more recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I don’t think this is totally brand new. I mean, it’s more obvious now because work has such specific expectations, like I’m supposed to sit there and move through claims in a steady way, but when I think back to school, there were definitely signs.\n\nIn elementary school especially, I remember teachers writing things like, I was bright or capable, but I’d lose worksheets, or I’d miss instructions, or I’d need reminders to actually finish the classwork. Like I wasn’t trying to be difficult, and I don’t remember thinking I didn’t care. It was more that I’d somehow not have the paper I needed, or I’d realize everyone else had started something and I hadn’t fully caught what we were supposed to do, or the teacher would have to come by and nudge me to finish.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it’s not like it appeared out of nowhere recently. It feels like the same kind of pattern, just now it’s causing bigger problems because I’m not a kid in a classroom anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you notice yourself frequently interrupting others, blurting out answers or comments before someone finishes speaking, or having trouble waiting your turn in conversations or lines?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… somewhat. I wouldn’t say I’m, like, pacing around the house all night or unable to relax in a dramatic way, but in situations where I’m supposed to sit still for a while, I definitely notice it.

Like in trainings at work, or longer team meetings, I’m usually doing something with my body even if I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention. I’ll be bouncing my leg under the table, or clicking my pen, or shifting around in the chair a lot. Sometimes I’ll catch myself doing it because I realize the pen clicking is probably annoying, and then I stop, but then I’m moving my foot or adjusting how I’m sitting instead.

It’s not that I’m trying to be disruptive or anything. It’s more like sitting there completely still feels hard to maintain, especially when the meeting goes on for a while. Socially, I don’t know that I talk way more than everyone else, but the fidgeting in meetings or trainings is definitely something I do.

Like, I’m trying to think of specific examples, and I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly interrupting people or blurting things out before they’re done.\n\nI mean, I probably have normal moments where I jump in because I think I know what someone’s saying, or I get excited and say something too soon, but not in a way that people are always calling me out for it or where it feels like a big pattern. And waiting in lines is annoying, sure, but I don’t think I have some major problem with it beyond just being impatient like anyone else sometimes.\n\nSo… yeah, compared to the desk and task stuff, that doesn’t really stand out to me as a major issue.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice yourself fidgeting a lot, feeling restless when you're supposed to be relaxing, or talking more than people around you in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… somewhat. I wouldn’t say I’m, like, pacing around the house all night or unable to relax in a dramatic way, but in situations where I’m supposed to sit still for a while, I definitely notice it.\n\nLike in trainings at work, or longer team meetings, I’m usually doing something with my body even if I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention. I’ll be bouncing my leg under the table, or clicking my pen, or shifting around in the chair a lot. Sometimes I’ll catch myself doing it because I realize the pen clicking is probably annoying, and then I stop, but then I’m moving my foot or adjusting how I’m sitting instead.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be disruptive or anything. It’s more like sitting there completely still feels hard to maintain, especially when the meeting goes on for a while. Socially, I don’t know that I talk way more than everyone else, but the fidgeting in meetings or trainings is definitely something I do.\n\n[Last Question]\nGoing back to when you were a kid, do you remember having that same kind of restlessness or fidgeting—like needing to move, tap, or shift around when you were supposed to sit still in class—or does that feel like something that's newer for you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / insurance_claims_or_policy_processing_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Fidgeting\nmanifestation: \"In trainings or longer team meetings, I’m usually bouncing my leg, clicking my pen, or shifting around in the chair even when I’m trying to look focused.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how far back it goes, honestly. Like, I can remember the school focus stuff more clearly than the physical restlessness stuff. I don’t have a super vivid memory of, you know, being the kid who was always getting in trouble for getting out of his seat or anything like that.

But the fidgeting part… I mean, it feels familiar, if that makes sense. Like even now, in trainings or longer team meetings, I’m usually bouncing my leg, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair, doing something with my hands or feet, even when I’m trying to look focused and not draw attention to myself. It’s not like I’m trying to be annoying, it’s just hard to sit there completely still for that long.

As a kid, I can’t say for sure if it was exactly the same or how much teachers noticed it. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. But the feeling of needing to move around a little when I’m supposed to sit still doesn’t feel totally new to me either. It’s just that I remember it more clearly now in work settings, because I’m aware of trying to look professional and not distract people.

I wouldn’t say I’m, like, pacing around the house all night or unable to relax in a dramatic way, but in situations where I’m supposed to sit still for a while, I definitely notice it.\n\nLike in trainings at work, or longer team meetings, I’m usually doing something with my body even if I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that one definitely happens. Probably more than I like admitting, honestly.

At work especially, it’ll be these small follow-up things that I fully intend to do, and then they just slip out of my head. Like I’ll mean to call a claimant back after I finish looking at something, or I’ll need to update a diary note, or send out a form that I already mentally told myself, “Okay, I’ll send that before the end of the day.” And then later I’ll realize I never did it.

It’s frustrating because it’s usually not that I don’t care or I’m blowing it off on purpose. In the moment, I really do think I’m going to remember. I’ll be like, “I just have to finish this other thing first,” and then I get pulled into the next claim or another email or whatever, and by the time I come back around, the original follow-up is just gone from my mind until something reminds me.

So, yeah, simple things like returning calls, updating notes, sending forms — those are the kinds of things I forget more often than I’m comfortable with.

I don’t have a super vivid memory of, you know, being the kid who was always getting in trouble for getting out of his seat or anything like that.\n\nBut the fidgeting part… I mean, it feels familiar, if that makes sense. Like even now, in trainings or longer team meetings, I’m usually bouncing my leg, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair, doing something with my hands or feet, even when I’m trying to look focused and not draw attention to myself. It’s not like I’m trying to be annoying, it’s just hard to sit there completely still for that long.\n\nAs a kid, I can’t say for sure if it was exactly the same or how much teachers noticed it. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. But the feeling of needing to move around a little when I’m supposed to sit still doesn’t feel totally new to me either. It’s just that I remember it more clearly now in work settings, because I’m aware of trying to look professional and not distract people.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Determine whether inattentive symptoms (e.g., losing track of tasks, forgetfulness\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one definitely happens. Probably more than I like admitting, honestly.\n\nAt work especially, it’ll be these small follow-up things that I fully intend to do, and then they just slip out of my head. Like I’ll mean to call a claimant back after I finish looking at something, or I’ll need to update a diary note, or send out a form that I already mentally told myself, “Okay, I’ll send that before the end of the day.” And then later I’ll realize I never did it.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s usually not that I don’t care or I’m blowing it off on purpose. In the moment, I really do think I’m going to remember. I’ll be like, “I just have to finish this other thing first,” and then I get pulled into the next claim or another email or whatever, and by the time I come back around, the original follow-up is just gone from my mind until something reminds me.\n\nSo, yeah, simple things like returning calls, updating notes, sending forms — those are the kinds of things I forget more often than I’m comfortable with.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find it hard to engage in quiet leisure activities—like reading or watching a show—without feeling restless or needing to get up, or do you often feel like you're 'on the go' even during downtime?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I don’t think I feel like I’m constantly “on the go” during downtime, at least not in a really obvious way.

Like, if I’m watching a show, I can usually sit through it, especially if I’m actually interested in it. I might change positions or look at my phone or get up to grab water or something, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as this strong inner restlessness where I can’t relax at all. Reading is harder for me sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s because I feel physically restless versus just not staying with it mentally.

So… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed that as a major thing outside of situations where I’m expected to sit still for a long time, like meetings or trainings. Downtime doesn’t usually feel like I have to be moving nonstop.

I don’t think so, not in a way that people have pointed out to me. I’ll be aware afterward, like, “Okay, I shouldn’t have left my desk, I need to get back to this,” but in the moment it’s like I drift away from the task and then I’m not where I’m supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, did you notice similar problems with staying focused or sticking to tasks when you were a kid or teenager, or is this something that's really only become an issue more recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I don’t think this is totally brand new. I mean, it’s more obvious now because work has such specific expectations, like I’m supposed to sit there and move through claims in a steady way, but when I think back to school, there were definitely signs.\n\nIn elementary school especially, I remember teachers writing things like, I was bright or capable, but I’d lose worksheets, or I’d miss instructions, or I’d need reminders to actually finish the classwork. Like I wasn’t trying to be difficult, and I don’t remember thinking I didn’t care. It was more that I’d somehow not have the paper I needed, or I’d realize everyone else had started something and I hadn’t fully caught what we were supposed to do, or the teacher would have to come by and nudge me to finish.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it’s not like it appeared out of nowhere recently. It feels like the same kind of pattern, just now it’s causing bigger problems because I’m not a kid in a classroom anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice yourself frequently interrupting others, blurting out answers or comments before someone finishes speaking, or having trouble waiting your turn in conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think that’s really one of the main things I’ve noticed, honestly. Like, I’m trying to think of specific examples, and I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly interrupting people or blurting things out before they’re done.\n\nI mean, I probably have normal moments where I jump in because I think I know what someone’s saying, or I get excited and say something too soon, but not in a way that people are always calling me out for it or where it feels like a big pattern. And waiting in lines is annoying, sure, but I don’t think I have some major problem with it beyond just being impatient like anyone else sometimes.\n\nSo… yeah, compared to the desk and task stuff, that doesn’t really stand out to me as a major issue.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice yourself fidgeting a lot, feeling restless when you're supposed to be relaxing, or talking more than people around you in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… somewhat. I wouldn’t say I’m, like, pacing around the house all night or unable to relax in a dramatic way, but in situations where I’m supposed to sit still for a while, I definitely notice it.\n\nLike in trainings at work, or longer team meetings, I’m usually doing something with my body even if I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention. I’ll be bouncing my leg under the table, or clicking my pen, or shifting around in the chair a lot. Sometimes I’ll catch myself doing it because I realize the pen clicking is probably annoying, and then I stop, but then I’m moving my foot or adjusting how I’m sitting instead.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be disruptive or anything. It’s more like sitting there completely still feels hard to maintain, especially when the meeting goes on for a while. Socially, I don’t know that I talk way more than everyone else, but the fidgeting in meetings or trainings is definitely something I do.\n\nDoctor: Going back to when you were a kid, do you remember having that same kind of restlessness or fidgeting—like needing to move, tap, or shift around when you were supposed to sit still in class—or does that feel like something that's newer for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how far back it goes, honestly. Like, I can remember the school focus stuff more clearly than the physical restlessness stuff. I don’t have a super vivid memory of, you know, being the kid who was always getting in trouble for getting out of his seat or anything like that.\n\nBut the fidgeting part… I mean, it feels familiar, if that makes sense. Like even now, in trainings or longer team meetings, I’m usually bouncing my leg, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair, doing something with my hands or feet, even when I’m trying to look focused and not draw attention to myself. It’s not like I’m trying to be annoying, it’s just hard to sit there completely still for that long.\n\nAs a kid, I can’t say for sure if it was exactly the same or how much teachers noticed it. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. But the feeling of needing to move around a little when I’m supposed to sit still doesn’t feel totally new to me either. It’s just that I remember it more clearly now in work settings, because I’m aware of trying to look professional and not distract people.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Determine whether inattentive symptoms (e.g., losing track of tasks, forgetfulness\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one definitely happens. Probably more than I like admitting, honestly.\n\nAt work especially, it’ll be these small follow-up things that I fully intend to do, and then they just slip out of my head. Like I’ll mean to call a claimant back after I finish looking at something, or I’ll need to update a diary note, or send out a form that I already mentally told myself, “Okay, I’ll send that before the end of the day.” And then later I’ll realize I never did it.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s usually not that I don’t care or I’m blowing it off on purpose. In the moment, I really do think I’m going to remember. I’ll be like, “I just have to finish this other thing first,” and then I get pulled into the next claim or another email or whatever, and by the time I come back around, the original follow-up is just gone from my mind until something reminds me.\n\nSo, yeah, simple things like returning calls, updating notes, sending forms — those are the kinds of things I forget more often than I’m comfortable with.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to engage in quiet leisure activities—like reading or watching a show—without feeling restless or needing to get up, or do you often feel like you're 'on the go' even during downtime?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… the clearest example that comes to mind is still work, honestly. That’s where it shows up the most because there are so many little follow-up tasks that all kind of stack on top of each other.

Like I’ll fully mean to return a claimant’s call, or I’ll look at a file and think, “Okay, I need to update that diary note,” or I’ll remember there’s a form I need to send out before the end of the day. And it’s not like I’m deciding not to do it — I really do intend to. But then I’ll move on to the next thing, or an email comes in, or I’m checking another claim, and somehow that original task just falls out of my head.

Then later I’ll see the file again or someone follows up, and I get that awful feeling of, “Oh, right, I never did that.” It’s usually simple stuff too, which makes it more frustrating, because it feels like it should be easy to just remember. But I forget those little follow-ups more than I want to admit.

Socially, I don’t know that I talk way more than everyone else, but the fidgeting in meetings or trainings is definitely something I do.\n\nDoctor: Going back to when you were a kid, do you remember having that same kind of restlessness or fidgeting—like needing to move, tap, or shift around when you were supposed to sit still in class—or does that feel like something that's newer for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how far back it goes, honestly. Like, I can remember the school focus stuff more clearly than the physical restlessness stuff. I don’t have a super vivid memory of, you know, being the kid who was always getting in trouble for getting out of his seat or anything like that.\n\nBut the fidgeting part… I mean, it feels familiar, if that makes sense. Like even now, in trainings or longer team meetings, I’m usually bouncing my leg, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair, doing something with my hands or feet, even when I’m trying to look focused and not draw attention to myself. It’s not like I’m trying to be annoying, it’s just hard to sit there completely still for that long.\n\nAs a kid, I can’t say for sure if it was exactly the same or how much teachers noticed it. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. But the feeling of needing to move around a little when I’m supposed to sit still doesn’t feel totally new to me either. It’s just that I remember it more clearly now in work settings, because I’m aware of trying to look professional and not distract people.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Determine whether inattentive symptoms (e.g., losing track of tasks, forgetfulness\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one definitely happens. Probably more than I like admitting, honestly.\n\nAt work especially, it’ll be these small follow-up things that I fully intend to do, and then they just slip out of my head. Like I’ll mean to call a claimant back after I finish looking at something, or I’ll need to update a diary note, or send out a form that I already mentally told myself, “Okay, I’ll send that before the end of the day.” And then later I’ll realize I never did it.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s usually not that I don’t care or I’m blowing it off on purpose. In the moment, I really do think I’m going to remember. I’ll be like, “I just have to finish this other thing first,” and then I get pulled into the next claim or another email or whatever, and by the time I come back around, the original follow-up is just gone from my mind until something reminds me.\n\nSo, yeah, simple things like returning calls, updating notes, sending forms — those are the kinds of things I forget more often than I’m comfortable with.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to engage in quiet leisure activities—like reading or watching a show—without feeling restless or needing to get up, or do you often feel like you're 'on the go' even during downtime?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I don’t think I feel like I’m constantly “on the go” during downtime, at least not in a really obvious way.\n\nLike, if I’m watching a show, I can usually sit through it, especially if I’m actually interested in it. I might change positions or look at my phone or get up to grab water or something, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as this strong inner restlessness where I can’t relax at all. Reading is harder for me sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s because I feel physically restless versus just not staying with it mentally.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed that as a major thing outside of situations where I’m expected to sit still for a long time, like meetings or trainings. Downtime doesn’t usually feel like I have to be moving nonstop.\n\nDoctor: Do people ever comment that you talk more than others in conversations or meetings, or that you have trouble stopping once you start talking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way that people have pointed out to me. I mean, I can definitely ramble sometimes if I’m talking about something I’m really interested in or if I’m trying to explain the whole background of something, because I don’t always know what details are important and what aren’t. Like even now I’m probably giving more explanation than you asked for.\n\nBut as far as people actually saying, “You talk too much,” or “You won’t stop talking,” I can’t really think of that being a common complaint. In meetings at work, I’m usually more on the careful side because I don’t want to say something wrong or waste people’s time. If anything, I might over-explain when I do speak up, especially if I’m nervous or trying to make sure I’m understood, but I don’t think I’m usually dominating the conversation.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say that’s a big thing people comment on.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice similar problems—like forgetting tasks, losing things, or feeling disorganized—affecting things like your home life, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the clearest example that comes to mind is still work, honestly. But then I’ll move on to the next thing, or an email comes in, or I’m checking another claim, and somehow that original task just falls out of my head.\n\nThen later I’ll see the file again or someone follows up, and I get that awful feeling of, “Oh, right, I never did that.” It’s usually simple stuff too, which makes it more frustrating, because it feels like it should be easy to just remember.   },

And it’s not like I’m deciding not to do it — I really do intend to. But then I’ll move on to the next thing, or an email comes in, or I’m checking another claim, and somehow that original task just falls out of my head.\n\nThen later I’ll see the file again or someone follows up, and I get that awful feeling of, “Oh, right, I never did that.” It’s usually simple stuff too, which makes it more frustrating, because it feels like it should be easy to just remember. But I forget those little follow-ups more than I want to admit.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave people close to you—like a partner, roommate, or family member—ever complained about your forgetfulness, disorganization, or restlessness affecting things at home, such as missing bills, chores, or appointments?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / insurance_claims_or_policy_processing_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness\nmanifestation: \"I forget simple work follow-ups more than I want to admit, like returning a claimant’s call, updating a diary note, or sending a form I meant to send earlier that day.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, people close to me have noticed the forgetfulness more than the restlessness, I think. I’m trying to think of home examples, but honestly the thing that stands out the most is still the work follow-up stuff.

Like I’ll mean to return a claimant’s call, or I’ll see that I need to update a diary note, or there’s a form I’m supposed to send out earlier in the day, and I genuinely intend to do it. It’s not like I’m blowing it off. But then I get pulled into something else, or I move on to another file, and it just kind of disappears from my mind until later.

And then when it comes back up, I feel embarrassed because it’s usually something simple. It’s not some complicated task. It’s just, “Call this person back,” or “send that form,” and somehow I still miss it. So if people are frustrated with me, it’s usually that kind of thing — me forgetting something I said I was going to do, more than being restless or anything like that.

That’s where it shows up the most because there are so many little follow-up tasks that all kind of stack on top of each other.\n\nLike I’ll fully mean to return a claimant’s call, or I’ll look at a file and think, “Okay, I need to update that diary note,” or I’ll remember there’s a form I need to send out before the end of the day. And it’s not like I’m deciding not to do it — I really do intend to. But then I’ll move on to the next thing, or an email comes in, or I’m checking another claim, and somehow that original task just falls out of my head.\n\nThen later I’ll see the file again or someone follows up, and I get that awful feeling of, “Oh, right, I never did that.” It’s usually simple stuff too, which makes it more frustrating, because it feels like it should be easy to just remember. But I forget those little follow-ups more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have people close to you—like a partner, roommate, or family member—ever complained about your forgetfulness, disorganization, or restlessness affecting things at home, such as missing bills, chores, or appointments?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, people close to me have noticed the forgetfulness more than the restlessness, I think. I’m trying to think of home examples, but honestly the thing that stands out the most is still the work follow-up stuff.\n\nLike I’ll mean to return a claimant’s call, or I’ll see that I need to update a diary note, or there’s a form I’m supposed to send out earlier in the day, and I genuinely intend to do it. It’s not like I’m blowing it off. But then I get pulled into something else, or I move on to another file, and it just kind of disappears from my mind until later.\n\nAnd then when it comes back up, I feel embarrassed because it’s usually something simple. It’s not some complicated task. It’s just, “Call this person back,” or “send that form,” and somehow I still miss it. So if people are frustrated with me, it’s usually that kind of thing — me forgetting something I said I was going to do, more than being restless or anything like that."
  }

Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"None\",\"name\":\"None\",\"description\":\"None\",\"reason\":\"The question is about impulsive symptoms such as interrupting, blurting out, or difficulty waiting turn, which are not covered by the provided symptom profile items.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not totally sure how far back it goes, honestly. I don’t have a super vivid memory of, you know, being the kid who was always getting in trouble for getting out of his seat or anything like that.\n\nBut the fidgeting part… I mean, it feels familiar, if that makes sense. It’s not like I’m trying to be annoying, it’s just hard to sit there completely still for that long.\n\nAs a kid, I can’t say for sure if it was exactly the same or how much teachers noticed it. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Probably more than I like admitting, honestly.\n\nAt work especially, it’ll be these small follow-up things that I fully intend to do, and then they just slip out of my head. Like I’ll mean to call a claimant back after I finish looking at something, or I’ll need to update a diary note, or send out a form that I already mentally told myself, “Okay, I’ll send that before the end of the day.” And then later I’ll realize I never did it.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s usually not that I don’t care or I’m blowing it off on purpose. In the moment, I really do think I’m going to remember. I’ll be like, “I just have to finish this other thing first,” and then I get pulled into the next claim or another email or whatever, and by the time I come back around, the original follow-up is just gone from my mind until something reminds me.\n\nSo, yeah, simple things like returning calls, updating notes, sending forms — those are the kinds of things I forget more often than I’m comfortable with.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t think so, not in a way that people have pointed out to me. I mean, I can definitely ramble sometimes if I’m talking about something I’m really interested in or if I’m trying to explain the whole background of something, because I don’t always know what details are important and what aren’t. Like even now I’m probably giving more explanation than you asked for.\n\nBut as far as people actually saying, “You talk too much,” or “You won’t stop talking,” I can’t really think of that being a common complaint. In meetings at work, I’m usually more on the careful side because I don’t want to say something wrong or waste people’s time. If anything, I might over-explain when I do speak up, especially if I’m nervous or trying to make sure I’m understood, but I don’t think I’m usually dominating the conversation.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say that’s a big thing people comment on.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
